Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F4515th of 19

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F4515th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structures under Volati's platforms are better than its share price and earnings imply, and worse than its own marketing implies.

Four of five platforms hold number one or two positions in fragmented or locally concentrated niches with rational pricing, low disintermediation risk and few new entrants: labels, masts, restoration and, regionally, concrete. Those niches produced about three-quarters of 2025 platform EBITA.

The structural damage is concentrated in grain handling. There, silo overcapacity from the 2020 to 2023 boom has triggered a price war that Tornum, with the group's leverage, cannot win by spending. Agricultural policy will extend the demand trough into 2027 to 2028. Concrete is a cyclical, rate-driven problem, not a structural one.

Suppliers and regulators are moderate headwinds. Competition for acquisition targets is intensifying just as Volati's capacity to bid is lowest.

For the model, take these points forward:

  • Treat Ettiketto, Communication and Corroventa as structurally sound, cyclically mixed businesses.
  • Treat S:t Eriks as a cyclical recovery candidate whose margin can return towards high single digits only when Swedish construction recovers.
  • Treat Tornum as structurally impaired for two to three years. Its 2027 to 2028 margins should be modelled near breakeven to low single digits, not back at the 7% to 10% target.
3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3235C-3350C-3440C-3740C-3847C-3940
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?F3518th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F5016th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Routes to market are mostly direct and neutral to helpful, with low disintermediation risk. Three specific intermediary dependencies add fragility: insurers and restoration contractors at Corroventa, public tenders at S:t Eriks, and large cooperatives such as Lantmännen at Tornum.

Reasoning.

  • Ettiketto embeds labelling machinery in customers' lines and sells direct under multi-year framework agreements (helpful: switching costs and repeat consumable orders).
  • Communication sells direct to mobile operators, tower contractors and, increasingly, defence and infrastructure buyers (neutral).
  • S:t Eriks sells infrastructure products direct to civil contractors through tenders, and landscaping lines through builders' merchants (neutral; timing risk around tender cycles; merchants are weak in a housing slump).
  • Tornum sells complete systems direct to farmers, cooperatives and processors (concentration risk: one cooperative project was over half of a year's sales).
  • Corroventa rents and sells to restoration firms whose spend is set by insurance claims (event-driven).
  • Experts consider disintermediation unlikely, because technical customisation, municipal standards and local service requirements protect channel margins.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F4017th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Competitors hurt Volati in grain storage and standard concrete, are neutral in labels and restoration, and are benign in masts. Volati's position is fragile only where it is weakest: Tornum, where it lacks the balance sheet to outlast a price war.

Reasoning.

  • In grain storage, global overcapacity and discounting (C-28, C-29) cut Tornum's margin to about 2% in Q2 and to about -3% LTM.
  • In concrete, local rivals pressure standard products, and S:t Eriks is ceding volume.
  • In labels, global converters pass costs through, and Ettiketto competes against regional printers it can buy.
  • In acquisitions, better-capitalised peers (Addtech, Indutrade, Lifco, Lagercrantz, and Momentum Group with 24% average acquired growth) can outbid a leverage-constrained Volati.
  • DNB's sector work places Volati among the names with the most severe downgrades over twelve months, alongside Bergman & Beving and Sdiptech. Beijer Alma, Asker and Idun led on upgrades.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F4018th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F4715th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Disruption acts on every actor around Volati, and in net terms it is mildly favourable. It burdens smaller converters and cement suppliers more than Volati, and it pressures farm customers more than Tornum's competitors. Overall it leaves Volati's position moderately robust in labels and concrete and fragile in grain handling.

Reasoning.

  • Demand side. Farmers face subsidy cuts and nitrogen limits (negative for Tornum). Brand owners face packaging regulation and redesign label formats (positive for capable converters). Municipalities face budget limits (negative for water and wastewater pipe).
  • Competitors. Small flexographic converters cannot fund digital presses or regulatory compliance and so become acquisition targets for Ettiketto (positive). Grain-equipment rivals face the same overcapacity as Tornum (neutral to negative).
  • Suppliers. Cement makers face ETS costs and pass them on (negative for S:t Eriks' input costs). European paper capacity is a recurring source of supply shocks.
  • Regulators. They are actively tightening packaging, carbon and agricultural rules. Political tariffs cloud Communication's international sales.
C-39Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?F4015th of 19full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
3b

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement45C-2455C-2555C-2655C-2750C-2840C-2935C-3150C-3558C-3655
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)F4515th of 19full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

D5511th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Four of Volati's five platforms are number one or two in narrow Nordic or European niches. Tornum is a mid-sized player in the European market as a whole. At group level Volati is a small serial acquirer, far below the leaders in its cohort.

Reasoning.

  • Ettiketto. A leading Nordic self-adhesive label converter, with about SEK 1.7bn of sales after Interket. It is small against the global converters CCL Industries, Multi-Color Corporation and All4Labels, so it ranks highly in the Nordics but not in Europe.
  • Corroventa. Described as having Europe's largest emergency drying rental fleet and a leading European position against Dantherm and Trotec.
  • Communication. Top Nordic share in masts through Scanmast; MAFI is a patented global niche supplier of mounting hardware.
  • S:t Eriks. One of the two leading Swedish regional producers of precast concrete and landscaping stone (with Benders), in markets where freight weight creates local oligopolies.
  • Tornum. About SEK 0.9bn of sales, against a European grain-handling market of EUR 1.3bn to 2.0bn a year in which "premium providers" (Ag Growth International, Cimbria, PETKUS and others) control 60% to 70%. That is a mid-single-digit share of Europe. Tornum is a Nordic leader but not number one in Europe, despite one transcript calling it a "dominant European market leader".
  • Group. About SEK 4.8bn of sales in 2026e, against Lifco (SEK 28bn), Storskogen (SEK 33bn) and Indus (EUR 1.7bn). Similar in size to Sdiptech (SEK 5.2bn).
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D5513th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Concentration differs sharply by platform. Labels are fragmented in Europe, concrete is locally concentrated, grain handling is concentrated among premium providers, and masts and restoration equipment are consolidated niches. The fragmentation in labels is the runway for Ettiketto. The concentration in grain handling has not prevented a price war, because overcapacity overrides structure.

Reasoning.

  • Labels. European self-adhesive labelling is "dominated by local printing shops". The global top players are large, but the regional market is long-tailed, with the top five well under 30% in most countries. That suits a consolidator.
  • Concrete. Freight weight makes markets regional, and within a Swedish region the top two to three can hold well over 30%.
  • Grain handling. Premium providers hold 60% to 70% (expert call), with a steep distribution.
  • Masts and restoration. Nordic masts are "highly consolidated"; restoration equipment is split between a few premium developers and smaller rental operators.
  • The market for acquisition targets. About 15,000 Nordic targets, with the top five Nordic compounders controlling under 5% of the regional SME market. Supply of targets is not the constraint; capital and integration capacity are.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

D5512th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Shares in Volati's niches are generally stable, and new entrants have taken little share. Two exceptions matter: silo overcapacity built between 2020 and 2023 in grain storage, and a wave of new acquirers competing for the targets Volati wants to buy.

Reasoning. Labels, concrete, masts and restoration show no evidence of new entrants taking share. Qualification barriers, local service, specifications and freight are high, and vertical-integration threats are rated low: paper mills will not move downstream, cement producers will not enter finished products within five years, and restoration contractors lack the manufacturing IP. In grain storage, capacity added during the boom has led to discounting by incumbents and lower-cost producers (Kepler Weber's commentary). The files hint at "uncommunicated market share erosion to lower-cost local suppliers" in standard concrete and commodity drying, where S:t Eriks and Tornum walk away from unprofitable volume. That is share loss by choice, and it should be modelled as lower volume with better mix. In acquisitions, Momentum Group, Röko, Tången, Auroora and InstallatørGruppen have entered, and Lifco, Sdiptech and Indus have moved into German sourcing.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5014th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. In labels, share consolidates towards scaled converters that can fund digital presses and meet packaging regulation, and Ettiketto is one of the consolidators. In concrete and grain handling, troughs push out under-capitalised producers, which favours survivors with balance sheets; Volati's platforms survive but are not the best funded. In the market for acquirers, capital and reputation consolidate towards the proven compounders (Lifco, Addtech, Indutrade), and Volati is not among the advantaged today.

Reasoning. Uncompetitive regional converters and under-capitalised precast producers exit during troughs (Business Foundations). Packaging regulation and hybrid presses accelerate that in labels. In acquirer competition, Volati has a reputation as a long-term owner (former Tornum CEO, former Salix CFO) and its targets prize permanence. But leverage limits its ability to bid, and DNB models it acquiring at 6% a year against 8% to 10% for the premier acquirers.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F4016th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The attitude is benign and margin-first in labels, masts and restoration. It is volume-first in grain storage, where prices are clearly falling with volume. It is mixed in concrete: price pressure on standard high-volume products, rational in specified infrastructure products.

Reasoning.

  • Labels. CCL passes resin and shipping costs through; competition is "rational and value-based" in niches where customers prize availability and service.
  • Grain storage. Competitors "engaged in aggressive price discounting to capture limited market volume"; Tornum's margin fell from 7% to 2% in a quarter, and Kepler Weber's gross margin fell 3.7 points. This is the clearest case of prices falling with volume.
  • Concrete. "Intense local pricing pressure on high-volume products". Peer Vestum reports industry-wide pricing pressure from weak private construction demand.
  • Restoration. No evidence of price wars; pricing rises in flood events.
  • General. Expert evidence describes a gross-margin floor of 30% to 40% for established regional operators, built on local service.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F3517th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: is it unpredictable and reactive, and would competitors launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The attitude is stable in labels and the specified niches. It has become unstable in grain storage over the last 18 months. That is where a "shark attack" on pricing is already under way, and where Volati's balance sheet makes it the weaker party.

Reasoning. In grain storage, behaviour moved from orderly pricing during the 2020 to 2023 boom to discounting once farm capital spending froze in 2025 to 2026. Over-built capacity has to exit before discipline returns (the Wisdom Library entries "Competitive intensity is entry minus exit" and "Money war and last man standing"). Tornum's responses (selectivity, in-house silos, cost cuts) are defensive; it cannot out-subsidise larger, better-funded rivals. In standard concrete, pressure intensified as housing starts fell, and S:t Eriks is choosing to cede volume. In labels, nothing suggests instability; the risk would be a large global converter pricing aggressively in Ettiketto's new UK, Dutch or German markets, where Interket's mix is "standard, price-sensitive" labels (rated medium-to-low plausibility).

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5014th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The industry structure is supportive for Ettiketto, Communication and Corroventa, which together produced about three-quarters of 2025 platform EBITA. It is unsupportive for Tornum and only moderately supportive for S:t Eriks. The support is moderately fragile. It depends on regulation, local service and specifications holding (likely), and on overcapacity in grain storage clearing (slow).

Reasoning. Labels are fragmented, consolidating and rational on price, with switching costs from embedded machinery: supportive. Masts are consolidated with patents: supportive. Restoration has a premium position and fleet scale: supportive, but its demand is random. Concrete has a distance moat but rate-sensitive demand and local price pressure: moderately supportive. Grain handling has overcapacity and discounting: unsupportive for 12 to 24 months. Weighting by normal-year EBITA, the structure is moderately supportive. Weighting by current EBITA, where Tornum and S:t Eriks contribute little, it is more supportive than the share price suggests. That supports the view that this is a cyclical trough with structural damage confined to grain storage.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?C587th of 19full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F5513th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Substitution is a low-to-moderate, slowly rising threat, concentrated in labels over the long term. It is not a factor for the 18-month model.

Reasoning.

  • Labels. Direct-to-can digital printing and RFID smart labels could reduce demand for pressure-sensitive labels. At the same time they create premium niches, which hybrid digital-flexographic assets help Ettiketto capture.
  • Concrete. Low-carbon binders are an opportunity for early adopters, not a substitute threat. Competing materials in landscaping are not evidenced.
  • Grain storage. On-farm alternatives exist, but demand is set by farm economics, not by substitution.
  • Drying. Buying instead of renting is a choice within Corroventa's own offer, which includes both sales and rental.
  • Masts. Small cells and satellites are a weak substitute.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

F4516th of 198 answers
Judgement45C-4148C-4235C-4355C-4452C-4538C-4640C-4750
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

F4516th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Volati's competitive advantages are genuine but local, and the group is less than the sum of its platforms.

Ettiketto has a widening, machine-embedded switching-cost moat and a consolidation playbook. S:t Eriks has a durable distance-and-specification moat in a cyclical market. Corroventa and Communication hold defensible niche positions. Tornum's advantage is narrowing under a price war. At group level there is no linking advantage: no shared customers, technology or purchasing. The holding's distinctive asset, a long-term owner's reputation and an incubation record shown by Salix's 11.6x return, is constrained by leverage and was partly transferred to Salix with Salix's management.

Pricing power is cost recovery with a lag, not real pricing. No scale economies are shared with customers, so there is no customer-led flywheel.

For the model, take these points forward:

  • Assume Ettiketto can sustain mid-to-high-teens margins and mid-single-digit organic growth, plus acquired growth when capital allows.
  • Assume Corroventa and Communication revert to their target-zone margins (15% to 20% and 11% to 14%) over a cycle.
  • Assume S:t Eriks and Tornum recover only with their end markets.
  • Do not assume group-level synergies or real price increases.
C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F4815th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Volati's moats are real but modest, and they sit at platform level. The strongest is Ettiketto's machine-embedded switching cost, moderate and widening through consolidation. Then come S:t Eriks' distance-and-specification moat (moderate and stable) and Corroventa's fleet scale and know-how (moderate and stable). Communication's patents and specifications are moderate. Tornum's custom engineering is weak and narrowing in the price war. There is no group-level moat: no shared customers, technology or purchasing. The holding company's one historical advantage, its reputation and capacity as a disciplined long-term buyer, is currently impaired by leverage.

Moats by type.

  • Customer-related.
  • Ettiketto: labelling machines installed in fast-moving consumer goods and medical-technology production lines, with tailored formulations and printer calibration, give high repeat consumable orders.
  • Corroventa: its products and Tramex meters require technical training, embedding them in restoration workflows.
  • Tornum: installations require civil engineering and automation software, a moderate switching cost.
  • Production-related. Proprietary designs and assembly; capex of about 1.4% to 2% of sales; gross margins of 35% to 55% at manufacturing stages.
  • Scale-based.
  • S:t Eriks: local plant density against freight weight.
  • Corroventa: the largest European emergency rental fleet.
  • Ettiketto: central sourcing in acquisitions, the source of its margin-uplift playbook.
  • Regulatory. Municipal concrete specifications; MAFI patents; the advantage of early compliance in low-carbon concrete and packaging.
  • Culture and knowledge. A decentralised entrepreneurial culture, co-ownership at platform level, and the Volati Academy and Management Programme (15 of 22 group CEOs promoted internally).

Direction.

  • Ettiketto: widening through consolidation and regulation.
  • S:t Eriks: stable.
  • Corroventa: widening slightly through IoT and Tramex.
  • Communication: stable.
  • Tornum: narrowing.
  • The holding's acquirer advantage: narrowing while leverage binds and competition for targets rises.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

F3518th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Volati is small in absolute terms. It had about SEK 4.3bn to 4.8bn of sales across about 70 operating companies; the files cite about 1,600 employees in one place, a figure not reconciled with the pre-spin headcount. Its scale confers niche-level advantages (local plant density, fleet size, consolidation buying power in Nordic labels). It confers no group-level advantage over competitors, and it is too small to absorb a cyclical shock without straining its balance sheet.

Reasoning. Against peers: Lifco SEK 28.3bn of sales across 275 subsidiaries; Storskogen SEK 33.1bn across 114 units; Indus EUR 1.7bn across more than 40 companies; Sdiptech SEK 5.2bn across about 33 units, the nearest in size. Against platform competitors: CCL in labels, AGI and AGCO's Cimbria in grain, Heidelberg Materials as a supplier. Scale does not give Volati purchasing power, because buying is decentralised by design. Its small size also means a central team that is compact but stretched across three new platform CEOs, an integration and a turnaround at once. And a market value of about SEK 1.9bn with average daily trading of USD 0.3m to 0.4m restricts its institutional investor base.

C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?D5512th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F5216th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Volati's offerings are demonstrably not inferior in four of five platforms, and leading in some niches: Ettiketto's integrated label-and-machine supply, Corroventa's fleet and IoT, MAFI's patented hardware and S:t Eriks' low-carbon concrete. At Tornum, inferiority is not proven, but it is losing bids on price. Customers there value price over Tornum's engineering in the current market.

Reasoning. The case against inferiority rests on four points: high customer retention at Ettiketto; Communication's 25% Q2 growth and new defence and solar orders; S:t Eriks' early-mover launches (evoZero, SuperPlattan with a 50% lower carbon footprint, Cemvision); and Corroventa's European leadership. Customers in these niches prize availability, local service and technical support over purchase price, and Volati's components are "essential but trivial in cost" for parts of the base. The evidence that Volati is not leading on value is at Tornum, where customers choose cheaper bids, and S:t Eriks' standard products, where customers push back on price. One gap: Ettiketto suffered material shortages in Q2 2026 that "restricted delivery capacity". That was a reliability lapse at the leading platform. It was temporary, but it is unlike Indus' BETEK, which won share by keeping supply secure.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F3815th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Demonstrable pricing power is limited to cost recovery at Ettiketto, and historically across subsidiaries in 2021 to 2023, with a one-to-two-quarter lag. There is no evidence of real price increases above cost. In the two cyclical platforms, pricing power is currently negative.

Reasoning.

  • Evidence of application.
  • Subsidiaries raised prices to offset 2021 to 2023 material and freight inflation.
  • Ettiketto implemented price increases in Q2 2026, effective in full in H2 2026, under framework agreements with index-linked mechanisms.
  • Corroventa captures emergency premiums in flood events.
  • Evidence of erosion.
  • Tornum: discounting pressure, and margins down to about 2%.
  • S:t Eriks: customer resistance in standard products and a deliberate retreat from them.
  • Ettiketto: its margin fell to 15% in Q2 as acquired, price-sensitive volume diluted it and cost increases arrived before price.
  • Peer contrast. Lifco's subsidiaries reportedly push 3% to 7% price increases in the first year after acquisition. Volati shows no such programme.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F4016th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Not at all as a strategy. Volati's model captures scale and integration benefits as margin: "the Ettiketto Way" lifts acquired businesses from 5% to 10% margins towards 20%. It does not pass them to customers. This is consistent with a margin-first acquirer model, but it means no scale-sharing flywheel builds customer loyalty over time.

Reasoning. Neither the messaging (margin targets: Ettiketto 20%, Corroventa 22%, Communication 14%, Tornum and S:t Eriks 10%) nor the culture (decentralised profit responsibility) refers to sharing savings with customers. Customer benefit is expressed as availability, local service and technical support, not lower prices. That is defensible in niches where price is a small share of the customer's cost. But it leaves Volati exposed in price-led downturns (grain, standard concrete), where no loyalty from lower prices has been banked.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

D5014th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Several mechanical forces will lift financial outputs over the next four to six quarters. They do not compound competitive advantage each period, and they are partly offset by mechanical drags: the fixed preference dividend, interest, amortisation and lower acquired growth.

Mechanical positives.

  • Easy comparisons from Q1 2027 (Tornum, S:t Eriks and Corroventa were all weak in H1 2026).
  • Full run-rate of cost programmes from H2 2026.
  • The end of S:t Eriks' self-inflicted under-absorption.
  • Integration programmes running over 24 months (Interket from January 2026, Clever from early 2025).
  • The full-year effect of Tramex (about SEK 18m of EBITA a year).
  • Non-recurrence of separation costs.
  • The dividend cut freeing cash.
  • Ettiketto's consolidation advantage, which accrues mechanically as small converters fail to invest. This is the one force that compounds.

Mechanical drags.

  • The SEK 64m preference dividend.
  • Floating-rate interest in a rising-rate cycle.
  • Amortisation rising with each deal.
  • Slower acquired growth than peers, which mechanically widens the gap to the compounders each year that leverage binds.

Net. These forces are enough to lift EBITA from the trough. They are not enough to make Volati a structural outperformer against its peers.

4b

Management quality

F4019th of 1912 answers
Judgement40C-5545C-5638C-5745C-57b55C-5825C-5945C-6050C-6135C-6222C-6345C-6442
Group judgement

Company - Management

F4019th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is capable and honest but not exceptional, and it is operating with too little room.

The CEO is a disciplined, finance-first allocator. His one great act, the Salix separation, created large value for the shareholders who kept both shares. But it handed RemainCo the cyclical assets, the preference capital and a leverage ratio at its ceiling. The operating record since 2021 is weak, and five consecutive EBITA misses against the only long-standing broker show that visibility is poor even from inside.

The new platform and finance leaders look strong on paper but are untested. Incentives are clean and lean, and ownership is aligned. Governance is founder-controlled and thinly independent, and it has shown control lapses.

For the model, take these points forward:

  • Haircut self-help timelines by one to two quarters, given the new leaders and the record of optimistic recovery calls.
  • Give little weight to the 15% target.
  • Treat a balance-sheet action (a disposal, or refinancing of the preference shares) as possible but unsignalled.
  • Expect the company to keep prioritising acquisitions over buybacks once capacity returns.
C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

F4517th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The stated ambition is large: at least 15% EBITA-per-share growth over a cycle, "doubling every five years", and "grow above targets" once markets normalise. That is at the level of the best Nordic compounders. But the ambition has not been re-based for a smaller, more cyclical and more leveraged RemainCo, and the resources behind it are much weaker than peers'. Ambition currently runs ahead of capacity.

Reasoning. The target has been missed in three of the last four years. The five-year average is 12%, falling from 17% in Q1 2026 as the LTM turned to -31%. Management openly calls this a "growth gap". Peers' comparable ambitions: Lifco 15% to 20% (delivered about 18% to 20% over a decade); Sdiptech 15% EBITA growth with ROCE above 15%; Storskogen 15% for 2025 to 2027 (2025 delivered -3%); Indus "EUR 3bn sales by 2030". Volati also confined its ambition ("grow within existing platforms, no new platforms"). That is more disciplined, but less expansive.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F3819th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The platform-level strategy is sensible: European label consolidation, Corroventa's diversification, S:t Eriks' shift towards higher margins and Tornum's selectivity. The group-level strategy is not great. It kept three cyclical platforms and the preference capital together under one balance sheet at the ceiling. That leaves the company unable to act counter-cyclically (buying cheaply in the trough) and dependent on one environment, falling rates and recovering construction and agriculture.

Reasoning.

  • Moat-widening. Present only at Ettiketto (consolidation, packaging regulation) and Corroventa (IoT, Tramex).
  • Growth as a priority. Stated, but constrained.
  • Dynamic strategy.
  • The best acquirers buy into troughs. Lifco bought Boscaro in a demolition trough, and Indus used holding liquidity to buy tungsten inventory and win share.
  • Volati cannot: its trough coincides with its leverage peak.
  • Its decision to run S:t Eriks' factories below demand to release cash shows a capacity to accept short-term pain, but it was driven by the balance sheet.
  • Strategic coherence. It is weak: there are no links between the platforms (C-12). The Salix separation was bold and value-crystallising for shareholders who kept both shares, but it left the weaker half with the harder strategy.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

F4518th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Andreas Stenbäck (CEO since 1 May 2021, previously CFO) is an above-average, finance-first capital allocator. Leadership Foundations places him at about the 65th percentile of Nordic compounder CEOs. He is not a great operator. His defining action, the Salix separation, crystallised exceptional value for shareholders who kept both shares. His operating record since 2021 is one of missed targets, rising leverage and a falling share price.

Reasoning.

  • Actions in his favour.
  • The Lex Asea distribution of Salix (11.6x ROIC, 25.4% IRR on the 2015 purchase), executed on schedule.
  • About 40 bolt-on deals since 2014 at about 7x EBITA historically.
  • Ettiketto's EBITA scaled from SEK 52m (2019) to SEK 215m.
  • The refinancing with Nordea and SEB was completed.
  • Plain admission of misses ("we have not lived up to our growth target since 2021").
  • Lean pay (SEK 5.2m, zero cash bonus) and 716,433 shares owned.
  • Actions against him.
  • Since 2021: EBITA-per-share growth below 15% each year except 2022; organic decline in 2023 to 2025; leverage up to the ceiling; the share down about 50% to 60% from its 2021 peak before the spin.
  • Interket bought at about 22x trailing EBITA.
  • Optimistic recovery calls: construction "would return to organic growth during 2025 to 2026".
  • No prior P&L ownership; his career before Volati was in corporate-finance advisory (MCF, Keystone).
  • Archetype. An "enabler" in capital allocation, not an operational force. He depends on platform CEOs, three of whom are new.
C-57b

How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

D5511th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Hiring well is a stated and evidenced priority, and the 2026 hires look strong on paper. But the team is untested inside Volati, it was assembled in one burst during a leveraged trough, and RemainCo lost its strongest operator (Martin Hansson) to Salix.

Reasoning.

  • Priority. The CEO says "Ensuring we have the best possible CEOs is one of our top priorities." Volati Academy has trained 126 leaders and the Volati Management Program 34 since 2015 (23 now in leading roles). 15 of 22 group CEOs were promoted internally.

Rikard Ahlin was redeployed to dedicated M&A.
  • Weaknesses.
  • Simultaneous transitions overload a compact centre.
  • The executive team grew from three to eight, which helps accountability but makes the team new.
  • Pay for other senior executives is aggregated (SEK 16.0m across four people), and the warrants carry no operational performance conditions.
C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

F2519th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Poorly resourced. The balance sheet is at its ceiling, capex is low, the industrial platforms were under-invested for years while capital went to Salix and Ettiketto, and there is little buffer for a further downturn. The business was not "built yesterday for resilience".

Reasoning.

  • Capex is about 1.4% to 2% of sales (SEK 131m in 2025, consolidated), defensible for an assembly-led model but leaving little reinvestment ahead of a recovery.
  • Acquisition spending collapsed in 2025 (FactSet SEK 92m) before the leverage-funded 2026 step-up.
  • DNB: "capital has historically been almost exclusively allocated to Salix and Ettiketto, causing M&A to stall in other platforms."
  • S:t Eriks had to run plants below demand to release working capital.
  • Headroom against the 3.5x leverage maximum is about 0.4x to 0.6x of EBITDA.
  • Fixed claims ahead of ordinary shareholders take about 40% to 45% of EBITA.
  • The decentralised model has no internal audit function; one platform's inventory error ran from 2016 to 2026 unnoticed.
  • Positives: a new committed facility; low customer concentration at group level; operating cash conversion of about 86%.

C-61

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

D4513th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Shareholder alignment is strong in incentives and ownership. Governance prudence is only moderate. Management is lean-paid, not self-serving, and the founders own large stakes. But the board has thinned, founder control is high, and several governance and control weaknesses have appeared at once. The prudence that counts most, balance-sheet prudence, has been subordinated to sustaining acquisitions near the leverage ceiling.

Reasoning.

  • Shareholder-first evidence.
  • CEO pay of SEK 5.2m with no cash bonus, bottom decile among peers (Lifco's CEO SEK 84.5m).
  • Warrants bought at market price (no free options); total dilution 1.34%.
  • The CEO holds 716,433 shares; the chairman 18.6m.
  • Equity raised only twice in 20 years.
  • The Salix value was distributed in kind, not trapped in the holding company.
  • Governance concerns.
  • Founders hold 62.9% of votes. The board is down to five, with three fully independent members (the chairman is non-independent as a founder; Maria Edsman as former Bokusgruppen CEO).
  • The co-founder resigned amid a personal tax investigation.
  • SEK 7m a year of related-party rents to a board member's companies.
  • Repeated co-investment share transactions with key individuals.
  • No internal audit function; an inventory valuation error that ran from 2016.
  • DNB, the main broker, was also the separation adviser.
  • Prudence. The separation left all the preference shares with RemainCo, so the weaker credit carries the expensive capital. Leverage has been run deliberately near 3.0x. The dividend was kept at SEK 2.00 for 2025 despite the strain. These are not imprudent individually, but together they leave little scaffolding against a further downturn.

Financial profile

F2819th of 198 answers

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F2819th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Economic strength is weak at group level, and the weakness is magnified by capital structure.

Margins are about half the peer median. Group returns on capital including goodwill (about 4% to 6%) are below the cost of capital, although returns on tangible capital at the platforms are healthy. The bridge from EBITA to ordinary EPS loses about SEK 215m to 235m a year to amortisation, interest and the preference dividend. Ordinary equity is only about 43% of enterprise value. Leverage sits at the top of its range with little headroom.

The redeeming features are real. There is a large, partly self-help margin-recovery opportunity (normalised EBITA of SEK 550m to 600m against SEK 262m LTM). Operating cash conversion is respectable. Capex needs are low.

For the model, take these points forward:

  • Build from platform revenue and margin to EBITA.
  • Deduct amortisation (about SEK 85m to 95m, rising with deals), net financial items (about SEK 65m to 85m, with sensitivity to the Riksbank path), tax at 22% to 23%, minorities of about SEK 6m to 10m and the SEK 64m preference dividend.
  • Make the leverage path an output of EBITDA, not of debt repayment.
  • Use incremental margins of 25% to 40% for S:t Eriks and Tornum volume changes.
  • Keep acquired growth near zero until leverage on the company's definition is below 2.5x.
C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F3018th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. RemainCo is generating negative EPS growth on low revenue growth that is mostly acquired. LTM EBITA was down 31% to SEK 262m. Continuing EPS was SEK 0.29 in Q2 2026, against SEK 0.68 a year earlier. Consensus expects 2027 EPS to rise about 66% on 7% revenue growth. The growth there is mechanical: a recovery from the trough, magnified by leverage and by the fixed preference dividend. It is not durable compounding. EPS behaves as a levered derivative of cyclical EBITA, with an elasticity of about 2x to 2.5x.

Reasoning.

  • Revenue. 2025 RemainCo sales grew 0.6% (SEK 4,282m to SEK 4,309m), with organic growth of -4% and Clever's sales added. H1 2026 grew 7% (organic -2%). Consensus is +11.5% for 2026 (mostly Interket), +7% for 2027 (the annualisation of Tramex and NicEtikett plus a small organic recovery) and +3.6% for 2028.
  • EPS.
  • Reported continuing EPS: SEK 0.57 in 2025, SEK 0.09 in H1 2026, SEK 0.11 LTM.
  • Brokers' bases: DNB adjusted SEK 1.98, 2.30 and 2.82 (2026 to 2028). Nordea SEK 0.62, 2.02 and 2.70. The FactSet consensus "growth" of -10%, +66% and +28% mixes these two bases with an unclear base year (FY2025 SEK 1.45).
  • Elasticity.
  • Each SEK 10m of EBITA is worth about SEK 0.10 of EPS. A 10% change in 2027 EBITA (about SEK 40m) moves EPS by about SEK 0.40, or 17% to 20%.
  • Fixed claims before ordinary shareholders: net interest of about SEK 65m to 80m a year, the SEK 64m preference dividend and SEK 85m to 90m of amortisation. Together that is about SEK 215m to 235m, against 2026e EBITA of about SEK 300m to 326m.
  • Durability. Low. The 15% EBITA-per-share target was missed in 2023 (+13%), 2024 (-17%) and 2025 (-18%), and the LTM is -31%. The 2027 rebound depends on Tornum moving from loss to breakeven, a modest S:t Eriks recovery, Ettiketto integration and normal weather at Corroventa (see C-67), each one of them cyclical or execution-dependent.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F3518th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The opportunity to deploy capital at high returns is ample: fragmented label and niche industrial markets, about 15,000 Nordic targets, Ettiketto's margin-uplift playbook. The capacity to deploy is small for at least 12 to 18 months. Free cash flow after preference and ordinary dividends is perhaps SEK 50m to 150m a year, and leverage is at the ceiling. Until the engine is visibly running again, the fair multiple should be that of a levered industrial (about 10x to 12x EV/EBITA), not a compounder (16x to 25x).

Reasoning.

  • Returns available. Bolt-ons at about 6x to 8x EBITA give an initial pre-tax return of 12% to 17%. With "Ettiketto Way" uplift, acquired margins rise from 5% to 10% towards about 20%, so the eventual returns are higher. Tramex (20% margin) is accretive at once. But Interket's price (about 22x trailing EBITA, C-18) shows that recent deployment has paid ahead for the uplift.
  • Capacity.
  • Consensus: free cash flow of SEK 171m (2027e) and SEK 213m (2028e), from operating cash flow of SEK 445m and 496m less capex of SEK 116m and 122m and other items.
  • Less the preference dividend (SEK 64m) and ordinary dividends (about SEK 45m to 55m), that leaves roughly SEK 50m to 100m a year in 2027 for debt reduction or acquisitions.
  • At 7x EBITA, that buys SEK 7m to 15m of EBITA a year, an acquired growth of 2% to 4% of EBITA, below DNB's 6% assumption.
  • Larger deployment needs EBITDA growth first, to create debt capacity under 3.0x.
  • Organic reinvestment. Capex is low (about 2% of sales), and the opportunities mainly lie in S:t Eriks' product mix and Ettiketto's presses.
  • Implications. Mid-term EBITA growth of 8% to 12% a year from 2027 is plausible: 2% to 4% organic, 2% to 4% acquired, plus margin recovery. But long-term compounding at 15% needs both a cyclical recovery and a balance-sheet reset. The fair multiple is capped until leverage is below about 2.5x and the engine shows two or three disciplined bolt-ons a year.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

F2519th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Group returns on total capital are poor. ROCE including goodwill is about 4% to 6% (DNB: 3.9% for 2026e, a 5% three-year average). That is below a cost of capital of about 9% to 10%, and in the bottom tier of Nordic serial acquirers (peers 13% to 18%). Returns on tangible capital at platform level are good to excellent: Communication 61% and Corroventa 30% ROCE excluding goodwill. The gap reflects acquisition premiums paid plus the cyclical trough.

Reasoning.

  • ROCE including goodwill. About 5% (three-year average, DNB) against a proven-peer average of 13% to 18%. DNB: 3.9% (2026e).
  • Return on working capital. DNB gives 6% (2026e) against Lifco's 110% and Addtech's 88%. The Risks report cites 53% on another definition. The definitions differ, so quote with care.
  • ROE. Adjusted ROE was 18% at Q2 2026 (pre-spin definition) against a 20% target, with a five-year average of 24%. Post-spin, the book equity base is small: consensus 2026e equity is SEK 802m. Net profit of about SEK 110m to 140m therefore gives an ROE in the mid-teens, but only because leverage and goodwill-heavy assets sit on a thin equity base. Return on tangible equity is likely negative or meaningless because goodwill probably exceeds equity; this is not disclosed in the files.
  • Post-tax return on new invested capital. Not disclosed. Rough evidence:
  • Interket at about SEK 494m for about SEK 22m of EBITA gives about 4% pre-tax at the start, rising to about 10% to 13% if margins reach 12% to 15%.
  • Tramex gives about 12% to 14% pre-tax at a typical 7x to 8x price.
  • Clever started near 5% margins.
  • So recent new capital earns at or below the cost of capital until integration succeeds. The historical incremental ROCE hurdle is about 13% (CEO).
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F4516th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Conversion of net income to cash is adequate, and better than the earnings trend suggests. But headline conversion flatters the cash that is actually free for ordinary shareholders. The 86% LTM "cash conversion" is an operating measure, and H1 2026 benefited from a one-off inventory release at S:t Eriks. On DNB's equity-relevant definition (free cash flow after interest, tax and leases over EBITA), normalised conversion is about 50% to 60%, against 75% to 80% for peers.

Reasoning.

  • Company measure. Operating cash flow over EBITDA: 102% (2023) and 104% (2024), consolidated with Salix; 88% (2025); 86% LTM at Q2 2026 (operating cash flow of SEK 307m), up from 72% the prior year. Q2 2026 operating cash flow was SEK 163m (from SEK 105m), driven by inventory reduction at S:t Eriks. Q1 2026 was SEK -70m (seasonal build plus capex).
  • DNB measure. Free cash flow to EBITA of about 50% normalised (about 60% in another note), against a peer median of 77% to 79%. The Change Forces report cites 53% against 79%.
  • FactSet. Free cash flow over reported net income of 1.3x to 1.6x historically (consolidated), and consensus of 1.2x to 1.4x. These ratios look high only because net income is depressed by amortisation; they do not mean the cash is abundant.
  • Working capital.
  • It is the main swing factor: seasonal builds in Q1, releases in Q3 and Q4.
  • S:t Eriks deliberately liquidated finished-goods inventory in H1 2026, which will not repeat once production is realigned.
  • The Change Forces work models net working capital rising to about 21.6% of sales in 2026e, behind peers' explicit working-capital programmes (Bergman & Beving, Alligo).
  • The Risks report flags elevated inventories at S:t Eriks and Tornum as signs of under-absorption.
  • Leverage reportedly rose to 3.1x at Q3 after the July acquisitions. That implies Q3 operating cash flow only partly offset the acquisition spend.
  • Uses of cash before ordinary shareholders. Preference dividend SEK 64m, interest SEK 65m to 80m, leases (not quantified in the files), capex of about SEK 108m to 122m (consensus) and earn-outs. Ordinary dividends were SEK 159m in 2026 on the pre-spin dividend, falling to about SEK 45m to 55m.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

F2519th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Operating margins are low and have fallen for three years. Continuing EBITA margin went from 10.5% (2023) to 9.1% (2024), 7.4% (2025), 5.6% (H1 2026) and 5.9% (LTM). That is about half the Swedish compounder median of 13% to 16%. The trend should turn up from H2 2026 on self-help, but the recovery that consensus models stops well short of 2023 levels: about 6.7% to 6.8% (2026e), about 8.0% (2027e) and about 8.7% (2028e).

Reasoning.

  • Gross margin. FactSet's "gross margin" (5% to 9%) is a classification artefact and should not be used. Business Foundations reports gross margins of 35% to 55% at manufacturing and assembly stages, and a 30% to 40% gross margin floor for established regional operators.
  • Platform EBITA margins.
Platform FY2025 Q2 2026 Target
Ettiketto 17.9% 15% 20%
Communication 10.1% 14% 14%
Corroventa 15.2% 3% 22% over cycle
S:t Eriks 4.2% (9.1% in 2022) 5% about 10%
Tornum low 2% (about -3% LTM) 10%
  • Drivers of the decline. Volume under-absorption at S:t Eriks and Tornum; price discounting in grain; dry weather at Corroventa; acquired lower-margin businesses at Ettiketto (Clever and Interket at about 5%); separation costs in central costs.
  • Peers. Storskogen 9.4% to 9.7%; Indus 8.5% normal (12.8% on the windfall); Sdiptech 20% to 21%; Lifco 22% to 23%. On the peer table's 2027 consensus EBIT margin, Volati is 6% against Storskogen's 8%.
C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F3515th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The operating earnings base (EBITA) is reasonably clean: there are no aggressive add-backs, and the inventory error was self-identified. The bridge from EBITA to ordinary EPS has three large leakages that make adjusted figures look much better than what ordinary shareholders keep. First, amortisation of acquired intangibles (about SEK 85m to 90m a year and rising). Second, net interest (about SEK 65m to 80m). Third, and the item most often missed, the SEK 64m preference dividend, about SEK 0.81 per ordinary share, roughly 35% to 40% of 2027 consensus EPS.

Reasoning.

  • Adjusted versus reported. FY2025 continuing EBITA of SEK 318m against EBIT of SEK 235m, a gap of SEK 83m of amortisation. Items affecting comparability were SEK -14m in 2025 (SEK -3m in 2024). Restructuring was SEK -20m in 2025. 2026 has restructuring costs at S:t Eriks and Tornum (taken in Q2) plus unquantified Salix separation costs (the CEO said EBITDA grew 4% in Q1 once adjusted for them).
  • Other items.
  • The S:t Eriks raw-material valuation error, building since 2016, cut opening 2026 inventory by SEK 19m. It went through the restated opening balance and not 2026 profit.
  • ERP and business-system development is capitalised and amortised over three to seven years (modest).
  • Long-term projects use percentage-of-completion revenue (Tornum, Communication), which carries some judgement risk.
  • A 2024 reclassification moved a SEK 3m earn-out discount effect to finance costs.
  • Tax. Reported at 23.3% to 23.8% (2023 to 2025, consolidated); one source cites 20% for subsidiaries. Use about 22% to 23% for RemainCo. The preference dividend is not deductible.
  • Minorities. Small, about SEK 6m to 10m (co-ownership at platform level).
  • EPS bridge for the second set. Ordinary EPS = (EBITA − amortisation − net financial items) × (1 − tax) − minorities − SEK 64m preference dividend, all over 79.4m shares. Quote broker "adjusted" EPS only after checking which of these lines it adds back.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F2019th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Financial leverage is material and uncomfortable. It is the single most important amplifier in the case. The ordinary equity is only about 43% of enterprise value once the preference shares are counted as the debt-like claim they are. Fixed financial claims take about 40% to 45% of 2026e EBITA before ordinary shareholders. And the company-defined ratio is at the top of its range, with about 0.4x to 0.6x of headroom to the 3.5x maximum.

Reasoning.

  • Claims ranking ahead of ordinary shares.
  • Net debt of about SEK 1.4bn to 1.5bn: SEK 1,766m of debt and SEK 291m of cash at Q2 2026 on FactSet, including leases.
  • Preference shares valued at about SEK 1.0bn.
  • Against a market value of about SEK 1.9bn at SEK 23.75, that gives an EV of about SEK 4.4bn.
  • Ratios.
  • Company: 2.9x (Q2), reportedly 3.1x (Q3).
  • DNB, including preference shares and leases: 4.1x (2026e), 4.5x to 4.8x elsewhere.
  • Peers: Lifco 1.1x to 1.4x; Sdiptech 2.5x to 2.8x; Storskogen 2.3x to 2.6x; Indus 2.0x to 2.3x.
  • Amplification.
  • A one-turn change in EV/EBITA on 2027 EBITA of about SEK 400m is worth about SEK 5 a share, about 21% of the price.
  • A 10% change in EBITA moves EPS by 17% to 25%.
  • 100bp on rates costs about SEK 14m pre-tax (about SEK 0.14 a share).
  • Comfort. Low.
  • A 12% to 17% fall in EBITDA would breach 3.5x. Whether 3.5x is a bank covenant or a policy cap is not established in the files; some sources call it the "strict 3.5x maximum covenant".
  • The new facility with Nordea and SEB (signed in June 2026) removes near-term refinancing risk.
  • Deleveraging will come mostly from EBITDA recovery, not repayment, because free cash flow after dividends is small (C-50).
  • The key reflexive link. Leverage falls only if EBITDA recovers. EBITDA recovers mainly with the cycle. So the balance-sheet risk and the operating risk are the same bet.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

5a

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F3814th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price will add about 1% a year to group revenue and nothing to the group margin in 2026 and 2027. In the two cyclical platforms the price-cost spread is negative: S:t Eriks is squeezed between rising cement and aggregate costs and falling concrete prices, and Tornum is in a price war its larger rivals are escalating. Ettiketto's 2026 price increases have recovered its cost inflation, which is visible in Q3, but they are cost recovery, not real price. From 2028 price becomes mildly positive for margin (about +10bp to +20bp a year) only as the cyclical markets tighten. No forecast line should rely on real price increases; this is a business that passes through costs with a lag in its best platform and gives price in its weakest two.

Reasoning, by platform.

Platform Price contribution to revenue Price-cost effect on margin Pass-through lag Evidence
Ettiketto +3% to +4% in H2 2026; +1% to +2% in 2027; about +2% a year after Neutral (cost recovery complete) 3 to 6 months under framework agreements with index clauses Legacy margin about 17.5% in Q3 2026 against about 17.7%; price increases in Q2 "in full effect in H2"
Communication +1% to +2% Slightly negative in 2026 to 2027 (steel safeguard from 1 July 2026, US tariffs) Fixed-price framework agreements, no steel indexation Q3 margin flat at 11%; Valmont, by contrast, has steel-index clauses
Corroventa +1% to +2% (Tramex list prices) Neutral Short Insurers and restoration contractors resist day-rate increases even in flood years
S:t Eriks 0% to -1% on standard landscaping products; +1% to +2% on specified infrastructure products Negative in 2026 to 2027, about -50bp to -100bp of platform margin Public tenders reprice slowly Swedish ready-mix prices -0.3% year on year while cement rose 3.8% and aggregates 7.9% (Morgan Stanley, 5 October 2026); Heidelberg pushing about +5% European pricing in Q3 2026 (Bernstein, 2 October 2026)
Tornum -3% to -5% in 2026; -1% to -2% in 2027; flat in 2028 Negative Project-by-project AGI pursuing commercial business "at more aggressive prices" (30 July 2026); Kepler Weber farm gross margin 16.1% against 20.8% (13 August 2026)

Why the cyclical platforms cannot hold price until their markets recover.

  • S:t Eriks faces concentration on both sides. Upstream, Heidelberg Materials is Sweden's only cement producer (Skövde and Slite), is consolidating clinker at Slite (19 March 2026), holds a new 30-year Slite permit (8 September 2026) and agreed on 7 October 2026 to buy NCC's Swedish and Norwegian aggregates and asphalt business for SEK 5.5bn. EU ETS rules imply European cement prices must rise about 27% by 2030 (Rothschild & Co Redburn, 24 July 2026). Downstream, Swedish public procurers buy on price (a former Ramboll regional director, 19 January 2026), and Benders is expanding in infrastructure products (Botnia Betong, 25 November 2025). The structural squeeze is therefore not only cyclical: unless S:t Eriks moves mix towards specified low-carbon products (evoZero, Cemvision), the price-cost spread stays negative into 2028. This is why I hold S:t Eriks at 4% in 2028 and 5.5% in 2030, not the 9% it made in 2022.
  • Tornum is a price-taker in an overcapacity market. AGI is consolidating its US plants and launching a standardised bin at a "highly competitive price point" (29 July 2026). In-house silo production at SIMEZA protects Tornum's cost, not its price. Tornum's 2024 margin collapsed to 0.2% from 9.1% in 2023, which shows it has no structural pricing power in a farm investment freeze.

Why Ettiketto's price is recovery, not real price. The question the first set left open (are the 2026 increases real?) is answered by Q3: the legacy margin is back to where it was, not above it. If the increases were real, the legacy margin would be rising while material costs are flat. A future test is whether prices hold when label-stock and freight costs fall; the 2022 to 2023 record suggests partial give-back. Upstream, UPM and other label-stock suppliers hold the stronger hand. I therefore model the legacy Ettiketto margin flat at about 20% (excluding Clever) and put all of Ettiketto's margin progression into integration.

The interplay that matters for the model. Price helps revenue by about SEK 45m to 50m a year (about 1%) but is margin-neutral, so its effect on EBITA is roughly the cost inflation it offsets. The risk is asymmetric: if food inflation returns in Sweden (Danske Bank expects it by year-end 2026), Ettiketto passes it on with a lag (a temporary margin dip), while S:t Eriks and Tornum cannot pass on energy, steel and cement increases at all.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7545P-0155
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D4510th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Operating leverage is high in the cyclical platforms and moderate in the group. Decremental and incremental margins at S:t Eriks, Tornum and Corroventa have run at about 25% to 60% of the revenue change. At group level, a 1% change in revenue moves EBITA by about SEK 12m to 15m, or 4% to 5%. That creates a wide range of 2027 EBITA outcomes: about SEK 320m to 480m on plausible volume paths, before any balance-sheet effects.

Reasoning, with evidence from the files.

  • Tornum.
  • Q1 2026: sales fell about SEK 108m and EBITA by SEK 27m (from SEK 12m to SEK -15m), a decremental of about 25%.
  • Q2 2026: sales fell SEK 21m and EBITA by SEK 13m, a decremental above 60%, because price cuts were added to the volume loss.
  • S:t Eriks. In Q2 2026, sales rose 1% but EBITA fell 57% (SEK 47m to SEK 20m) because production was cut below sales. Under-absorption from production matters as much as sales. The margin fell from 9.1% (2022) to 4.2% (2025).
  • Corroventa. In Q2 2026, sales fell 14% and EBITA from SEK 4m to SEK 2m. Its rental fleet is a fixed cost.
  • Communication. In Q2 2026, sales rose SEK 40m and EBITA SEK 10m, an incremental of about 25%.
  • Group. From 2023 to 2025, EBITA fell by about SEK 154m on sales down about SEK 186m. Acquired, lower-margin sales were added in that period, so this overstates the true decremental, but it shows the scale.
  • Sensitivities (Change Forces, calculated). Revenue ±1 point moves EPS about ±1.7%. EBITA margin ±50bp moves EPS about ±11.6%. Volume ±2 points moves EPS about ±7.2%.
  • Range for 2027 EBITA.
  • About SEK 320m to 350m: S:t Eriks stays at about 2%, Tornum loss-making, Corroventa dry.
  • About SEK 380m to 410m: consensus-like.
  • About SEK 450m to 480m: S:t Eriks at about 6% and Tornum at about 4% if construction and agriculture begin to recover in 2027.
  • A full normalisation (S:t Eriks at 9%, Tornum at 7% to 10%) would add a further SEK 70m to 120m, but not before 2028.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C+555th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Operating leverage will lift EBITA strongly in 2027, by about SEK 80m (+25%), but for an unusual reason. Almost none of the lift comes from volume. It comes from removing self-inflicted under-absorption at S:t Eriks, from cost programmes that lower break-even points at S:t Eriks and Tornum, and from integration and full-year acquisitions at Ettiketto and Corroventa. Organic revenue growth in 2027 is only about 2%. Volume-driven operating leverage, which is what made 2023 to 2026 so painful on the way down (group EBITA from SEK 472m to SEK 318m on revenue down only about 4%), only starts working upwards in 2028 to 2030, and only if Swedish construction and European farm investment recover. The fixed-cost base therefore makes 2027 more predictable than the headline growth suggests, and 2028 to 2030 less predictable.

Reasoning.

1. How large the fixed-cost leverage is, by platform (evidence from 2026).

  • S:t Eriks. The best evidence is the asymmetry between production and sales. In Q2 2026, sales rose 1% but EBITA fell from SEK 47m to SEK 20m because plants ran below demand to release inventory: about SEK 25m to 30m of under-absorption in one quarter, plus about SEK 8m in Q1. That cost is now reversing: production was raised in Q3 and the margin held at 5% on sales down 9%. Heavyside building materials carry about 30% fixed costs (Deutsche Bank, 17 January 2025). I use incremental margins of 30% to 35% on volume at S:t Eriks once production is aligned, so each 10% of volume (about SEK 120m) is worth about SEK 35m to 40m of EBITA.
  • Tornum. Decremental margins were about 25% in Q1 2026 and above 60% in Q2 (volume plus price), but only about 13% in Q3 (sales down SEK 38m, EBITA down SEK 5m). The cost programme is visibly lowering the break-even point. Incremental margins on recovery will be lower than the decremental margins on the way down, because price will lag volume in a market with overcapacity (P-04). I use 20% to 25%.
  • Corroventa. The rental fleet is a fixed cost. Weather swings move the legacy margin between about 3% (Q2 2026, dry) and over 20% (flood years). Tramex (about 20% margin, about a third of Q3 sales) halves the weather sensitivity of the platform margin.
  • Communication. About 25% incremental (Q2 2026: sales +SEK 40m, EBITA +SEK 10m), but volume is lumpy and Q2 2026 is a tough comparison for Q2 2027.
  • Ettiketto. Low fixed-cost leverage, high materials share. Margin moves with mix and integration, not volume.

2. How it shapes 2027: the step-up is mechanical, not cyclical. Adjusted EBITA rises from about SEK 318m to about SEK 398m:

Source of the 2027 increase SEK m Type
Ettiketto: Interket and Clever integration, co-location savings from Q1 2027, about 3% organic +26 Self-help
Corroventa: Tramex for a full year (+10), partial weather normalisation (+6) +16 Acquired, random
S:t Eriks: no repeat of H1 2026 under-absorption, full-year cost programme, sales -1% +16 Mechanical
Tornum: cost programme lapping, easy H1 comparison, sales +3% +16 Mechanical
Head office, new bolt-ons, Communication flat +6 Mixed
Total +80

About 60% to 65% is mechanical or self-help within management's control, about 20% is the annualisation of acquisitions already made, and about 15% (Corroventa weather and Tornum volume) is outside control. Volume leverage contributes almost nothing, because I assume no end-market recovery before H2 2027 (as the first set advised).

3. How it shapes 2028 to 2030: volume leverage returns, and it is concentrated. From 2028 the drivers change. The S:t Eriks margin goes from 3.4% (2027) to 5.5% (2030) and Tornum from -1% to 3%, which together add about SEK 65m. New bolt-ons add about SEK 55m by 2030. Ettiketto adds about SEK 55m more. Of the SEK 200m of EBITA growth from 2027 to 2030, about a third depends on the cycle at S:t Eriks and Tornum, about a quarter on acquisitions not yet made, and most of the rest on Ettiketto's integration. The cyclical third is where operating leverage cuts both ways.

4. Against the last four to six years.

2023 2024 2025 2026e 2027e 2028e 2030e
EBITA margin 10.5% 9.1% 7.4% 6.7% 8.1% 8.9% 10.1%

The base case gets back to the 2023 margin only in 2030, and with S:t Eriks (5.5%) and Tornum (3%) still well below their earlier peaks (S:t Eriks 9.1% in 2022, Tornum 9.1% in 2023). That is deliberately cautious: the margin structure has changed because Ettiketto now carries two acquired businesses at 6% to 10% margins, and because the cyclical platforms face a price war (Tornum) and a negative price-cost spread (S:t Eriks).

5. The width of outcomes that operating leverage creates. For 2027 adjusted EBITA: bear about SEK 330m (S:t Eriks at 2%, Tornum SEK -25m, Interket stuck near 6%, Corroventa dry), base SEK 398m, bull about SEK 450m (S:t Eriks 5%, Tornum +2%, wet autumn and winter). For 2028: bear about SEK 370m, base SEK 462m, bull about SEK 540m. The cone widens sharply from 2028 because that is when volume leverage takes over from self-help.

6. What the peer precedents say about timing. Ag Growth International needed nearly two years to restore margins after the 2015 to 2017 downturn, Kepler Weber restored gross margins in 2016 by cutting capacity after a 38% revenue fall, and AGCO's grain storage sales stayed depressed in 2020 while farm equipment recovered ("grain storage investments recover slower"). Storskogen needed several quarters before its 1,000-person cut in Services showed in group margins. Volati's management said its programmes would show within six to nine months (Q1 2026 call); Q3 2026 at S:t Eriks (margin flat at 5% on sales down 9%) is the first evidence that they have.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7620P-0250
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F2019th of 19full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F5015th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage will amplify the 2027 EBITA recovery roughly twofold in EPS (+25% EBITA becomes +50% adjusted EPS, from SEK 1.49 to SEK 2.24) and then fade as an amplifier. The amplifier is not mainly debt. It is the fixed SEK 64m preference dividend plus about SEK 70m of interest, which together are fixed claims that do not grow with EBITA. The balance sheet repairs faster than the first set feared: on the company's definition, leverage falls from 3.1x at Q3 2026 to about 2.7x at end-2026 and about 2.1x at end-2027, driven by EBITDA growth (about half), the ordinary dividend cut (about a quarter) and free cash flow. That opens acquisition capacity from H2 2027. But on a definition that counts the preference shares as debt, leverage only falls from about 4.5x to about 3.3x by 2028, so the preference shares remain the binding constraint on capital structure to the end of the forecast.

Reasoning.

1. The claims ahead of ordinary EPS.

Fixed claim (SEK m) 2026e 2027e 2028e 2030e
Net financial items -71 -73 -69 -65
Preference dividend (1,603,774 shares at SEK 40) -64 -64 -64 -64
Minorities -10 -10 -11 -13
Fixed claims as % of adjusted EBITA 46% 37% 31% 24%
Amortisation of acquired intangibles (reported EPS only) -100 -108 -112 -121

EPS elasticity to EBITA (percentage change in adjusted EPS per 1% change in adjusted EBITA, measured at each year's level) is about 2.1x in 2026, 1.7x in 2027, 1.6x in 2028 and 1.4x in 2030. The 2026 to 2027 move (+25% EBITA, +50% EPS) is about 2x. The first set's rule of thumb holds: SEK 10m of EBITA is worth about SEK 0.10 of EPS (SEK 0.098 at a 22.5% tax rate).

2. The interest line. Net debt excluding leases was SEK 1,331m at Q3 2026, on a new facility with Nordea and SEB from 15 June 2026. DNB models a constant 4.5% borrowing rate. I use 4.6% for 2027 and 2028 (Riksbank hiking bias from 1.75%), plus about SEK 12m of lease interest and about SEK 5m of other items (earn-out discounting, fees). H1 2026 continuing net financial items were flattered because external interest was allocated to the discontinued Salix operations; the true continuing run-rate is about SEK 18m to 19m a quarter from Q3 2026. Sensitivity: each 100bp on floating debt is about SEK 12m to 13m pre-tax, about SEK 0.12 of EPS (the annual report's SEK 17m post-tax was on the larger pre-spin debt).

3. The deleveraging path, and why it is faster than the first set assumed.

Company definition Q3 2026 End-2026e End-2027e End-2028e End-2030e
Net debt excluding leases 1,331 1,222 1,129 1,110 1,101
EBITDA after lease costs (adjusted) c.430 c.458 c.547 c.621 c.778
Leverage 3.1x 2.7x 2.1x 1.8x 1.4x
Including preference shares (SEK 1.0bn) and leases, on IFRS 16 EBITDA c.4.8x c.4.5x c.3.7x c.3.3x c.2.7x

Three drivers:

  • Seasonal Q4 cash. Q4 releases working capital (about SEK +70m in my estimate), so Q4 2026 free cash flow after the preference dividend is about SEK 110m.
  • The dividend cut. The SEK 2.00 ordinary dividend for 2025 cost about SEK 159m in 2026. A dividend of about SEK 0.30 for 2026 (paid in 2027) costs about SEK 24m. That alone frees about SEK 135m in 2027, a quarter of a turn of leverage.
  • EBITDA growth. EBITDA after lease costs rises about 19% in 2027, which on its own takes about 0.4x off the ratio.

The first set said leverage below 2.5x was three to six quarters away. My base case crosses 2.5x around Q1 to Q2 2027, at the short end of that range. The risk to this path is the cash conversion of 93% over the last twelve months: it was flattered by the one-off inventory release at S:t Eriks, and with production back up, working capital will absorb cash in 2027 (I assume about SEK 40m). DNB's "normal" conversion is 50% to 60%; mine is about 58% of EBITA in 2027 (free cash flow before the preference dividend of SEK 231m over adjusted EBITA of SEK 398m), at the top of that range.

4. What the balance sheet allows, and when. At 2.1x by end-2027 and 1.8x by end-2028, the company has room for about SEK 300m to 500m of extra debt within its 2.0x to 3.0x range. I assume only SEK 50m of acquisitions in 2027 and SEK 150m to 200m a year from 2028, which keeps leverage falling. That is deliberately conservative: management has said it is comfortable at the top of the range, so the realistic risk is that it spends more, faster. Each SEK 100m of bolt-ons at about 8x EBITA adds about SEK 12m of EBITA and about SEK 0.06 to 0.07 of EPS after interest.

5. The preference shares: the one structural lever. The redemption price is SEK 625 a share after the tenth anniversary in May 2025, so redeeming all 1,603,774 shares costs about SEK 1.0bn, close to FactSet's implied market value of about SEK 994m. The dividend costs 6.4% a year, after tax, equivalent to about 8.2% pre-tax against about 4.5% for bank debt. Redeeming with debt would add about SEK 0.36 a share to EPS (saving SEK 64m against about SEK 35m of post-tax interest), but would add about 1.6x to the company's leverage. On my path that becomes feasible only around 2029 to 2030, or in part (say a third, SEK 330m, worth about SEK 0.12 a share) from 2028. Sdiptech redeemed its preference shares in March 2026 (1,750,000 shares, SEK 184m), but that was less than 5% of its financing; Volati's are about 40% of the claims ahead of ordinary equity. I do not put a redemption in the base case; it is the clearest unmodelled upside to EPS from 2028.

6. Where the balance-sheet risk now sits. Not in the covenant on the base case: the 3.5x maximum would need EBITDA after lease costs about 25% below my end-2026 figure, or about 40% below my 2027 figure. The residual risk is a year-end 2026 goodwill impairment at Tornum (goodwill SEK 139m at end-2025, return on capital employed excluding goodwill -14% over the last twelve months), which would cut book equity (2026e consensus SEK 802m) by up to about 17%. It is non-cash and does not touch the company's leverage definition, but it would hurt sentiment and any equity-based covenant (none is disclosed).

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7742P-0358
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

F4218th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Incremental scope is moderate: perhaps SEK 30m to 60m of further savings beyond the programmes already under way, mainly central costs and procurement. Large structural cuts are limited without plant closures or disposals at S:t Eriks and Tornum, and those would shrink revenue too.

Reasoning, by cost line.

  • Raw materials and consumables. The largest line: SEK 5,047m in 2025 on the consolidated group, about 60% of sales; the RemainCo split is not given. Pass-through and procurement can help, but decentralised purchasing limits pooled savings. Storskogen saved SEK 20m a year from joint electricity agreements and cut material costs about 8% in pooled purchasing at installation subsidiaries, showing what pooling can do. Volati has no such programme.
  • Personnel. Cut where possible. S:t Eriks and Tornum made headcount reductions in H1 2026. Collective wage growth of about 3% to 4% is sticky.
  • Other external costs. SEK 621m in 2025, consolidated. Some discretionary scope.
  • Central costs. About SEK 15m to 30m a quarter in 2026, including separation costs. The parent organisation was sized for a group twice the size, so it is a natural target. It is also lean by peer standards (about a five-person executive team before expansion), so the saving is perhaps SEK 10m to 30m a year.
  • Plant fixed costs (S:t Eriks precast, Tornum fabrication). Reducible only through capacity rationalisation or mix change. S:t Eriks is moving towards lower-capital products. Tornum's SIMEZA integration raises its fixed-cost base, not lowers it.
  • Uncuttable. The preference dividend, amortisation and lease costs.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

B583rd of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. About SEK 70m of EBITA from cost and efficiency measures is likely to reach the P&L by 2028, against 2026. About SEK 45m to 50m of it arrives in 2027 and is already largely visible in the Q3 2026 run-rate or contracted through the Ettiketto co-location. The remainder, mostly the Interket and Clever margin programmes, arrives through 2028. A further SEK 47m of 2026 reported EBITA is lost to one-off items that do not recur, which lifts reported EBITA by more than adjusted EBITA in 2027. Central-cost savings are unlikely to contribute, contrary to the first set. Large structural cuts (plant closures at S:t Eriks or Tornum) are not signalled, and I do not model them.

Reasoning.

Measure Status (evidence and date) EBITA effect against 2026 Timing
S:t Eriks cost programme and production realignment Launched Q1 2026; restructuring in Q2; "gradually increasing effect" (Q3 report, 23 October 2026); margin held at 5% in Q3 on sales -9% +SEK 16m in 2027 (of which about SEK 10m is no repeat of the H1 2026 under-absorption, about SEK 6m full-year savings) H1 2027, mostly Q1 and Q2
